What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you for seeing me. When I'm alone working late, I start eating and it feels like I can't stop or control how much I'm eating until it's over. It's just really starting to worry me.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating large amounts happen only when you are alone and working late, or do they also happen in other situations? Also, do you ever try to make up for them by vomiting, using laxatives, fasting, or exercising?\n\nPatient: They mostly happen when I'm alone, and I'll actually wait until my roommate goes to bed so I can eat in my room without her seeing, sometimes even after I've already had a normal dinner. And have they been going on for at least 3 months?\n\nPatient: These episodes have been happening at least once a week, and sometimes more often when I have big work deadlines. This whole pattern has been going on for about ten months now, starting shortly after I got promoted to senior designer.\n\nDoctor: When these episodes happen, do you eat a clearly large amount of food in a short time, or is it more that you feel out of control while eating a normal amount?\n\nPatient: It is definitely a very large amount of food eaten in a short window of time. I'll often order enough takeout for three people—like two entrees, sides, and dessert—and eat it all along with snacks within an hour or two. Once I start, a switch just flips and I feel completely out of control, like I can't stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about your weight or body shape, or ever try to restrict food, fast, or exercise because of how you feel about your body?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really focus on my weight or try to restrict food or exercise to compensate. These episodes are much more driven by my work stress and feeling completely out of control, rather than concerns about my body image.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods between these episodes when you purposely eat very little or skip meals because you are afraid of losing control again?\n\nPatient: No, I don't purposely restrict my food or skip meals out of fear of losing control. Any skipped meals are usually just because I'm so busy with work and lose track of time, rather than an attempt to prevent another episode.\n\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, do you feel a strong sense of distress, guilt, or shame while they are happening or right afterward?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I feel an overwhelming amount of shame and distress, especially right after it happens. I'm so embarrassed that I hide my eating from my roommate, and the next day the guilt is so bad that I end up canceling plans and just hiding in my apartment.

When I'm alone working late, I start eating and it feels like I can't stop or control how much I'm eating until it's over. It's just really starting to worry me.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating large amounts happen only when you are alone and working late, or do they also happen in other situations? Also, do you ever try to make up for them by vomiting, using laxatives, fasting, or exercising?\n\nPatient: They mostly happen when I'm alone, and I'll actually wait until my roommate goes to bed so I can eat in my room without her seeing, sometimes even after I've already had a normal dinner. I don't ever try to throw up, use laxatives, or over-exercise to make up for it; I just feel incredibly sluggish and ashamed the next day.\n\nDoctor: About how often do these episodes happen, for example how many times per week or month? I'll often order enough takeout for three people—like two entrees, sides, and dessert—and eat it all along with snacks within an hour or two. Once I start, a switch just flips and I feel completely out of control, like I can't stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about your weight or body shape, or ever try to restrict food, fast, or exercise because of how you feel about your body?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really focus on my weight or try to restrict food or exercise to compensate. These episodes are much more driven by my work stress and feeling completely out of control, rather than concerns about my body image.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods between these episodes when you purposely eat very little or skip meals because you are afraid of losing control again?\n\nPatient: No, I don't purposely restrict my food or skip meals out of fear of losing control. Any skipped meals are usually just because I'm so busy with work and lose track of time, rather than an attempt to prevent another episode.\n\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, do you feel a strong sense of distress, guilt, or shame while they are happening or right afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel an overwhelming amount of shame and distress, especially right after it happens. I'm so embarrassed that I hide my eating from my roommate, and the next day the guilt is so bad that I end up canceling plans and just hiding in my apartment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a significant fear of gaining weight or strong concerns about your body shape or size, even if you do not act on them?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had a significant fear of gaining weight or any major concerns about my body shape. The real distress for me is just the feeling of completely losing control in the moment and the heavy shame that comes right after."

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
